# International Criminal Court

Not to be confused with the [International Court of Justice](14918.md).

The **International Criminal Court** (**ICC**) is an [intergovernmental organisation](18201224.md) and [international tribunal](433437.md) seated in [The Hague](30269.md), Netherlands. Established in 2002 under the multilateral [Rome Statute](68762.md), the ICC is the first and only permanent international court with [jurisdiction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jurisdiction) to prosecute individuals for the [international crimes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_criminal_law) of [genocide](12441.md), [crimes against humanity](61099.md), [war crimes](48626.md), and the [crime of aggression](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crime_of_aggression). The ICC is intended to complement, not replace, national judicial systems; it can exercise its jurisdiction only when national courts are unwilling or unable to prosecute criminals. It is distinct from the [International Court of Justice](14918.md), an organ of the [United Nations](31769.md) that hears disputes between states.

The ICC can generally exercise jurisdiction in cases where the accused is a national of a state party, the alleged crime took place on the territory of a state party, or a situation is referred to the Court by the [United Nations Security Council](31956.md). As of October 2024, there are 125 states parties to the Rome Statute, which are represented in the court's governing body, the Assembly of States Parties. Countries that are not party to the Rome Statute and do not recognise the court's jurisdiction include [China](5405.md), [India](14533.md), [Russia](25391.md), and the [United States](3434750.md).

The [Office of the Prosecutor](33903993.md) has opened investigations into over a dozen situations and conducted numerous preliminary examinations. The Court issued its first [arrest warrants](146746.md) in 2005,[^cite_note-LRA_warrants-2] and issued its first judgment in 2012.[^cite_note-\:5-3] [Indicted individuals](18248714.md) have included heads of state and other senior officials. In recent years, notable cases include arrest warrants issued for Russian president [Vladimir Putin](32817.md), in connection with the [invasion of Ukraine](79400268.md), and Israeli prime minister [Benjamin Netanyahu](56472.md) and defence minister [Yoav Gallant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yoav_Gallant), along with several [Hamas](13913.md) leaders, in connection with the [Gaza genocide](75668427.md).

Since its establishment, the ICC has faced significant criticism. Opponents, including major powers that have not joined the court, question its legitimacy, citing concerns over national sovereignty and alleging susceptibility to political influence. The court has also been accused of bias and of disproportionately targeting African leaders. Others have questioned the court's effectiveness, pointing to its reliance on state cooperation for arrests, its relatively small number of convictions, and the high cost of its proceedings.

## History



The premises of the International Criminal Court in The Hague, Netherlands. The ICC moved into this building in December 2015.

### Background

The establishment of an [international tribunal](433437.md) to judge political leaders accused of international crimes was first proposed during the [Paris Peace Conference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paris_Peace_Conference,_1919) in 1919 following the [First World War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_World_War) by the [Commission of Responsibilities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commission_of_Responsibilities).[^cite_note-4][^cite_note-5] The issue was addressed again at a conference held in [Geneva](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geneva) under the auspices of the [League of Nations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/League_of_Nations) in 1937, which resulted in the conclusion of the first convention stipulating the establishment of a permanent international court to try acts of international terrorism.[^cite_note-\:1-6] The convention was signed by 13 states, but none ratified it, so the convention never entered into force.[^cite_note-7]

Following the [Second World War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_World_War), the [allied powers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Allies_of_World_War_II) established two *[ad hoc](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ad_hoc)* tribunals to prosecute [Axis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Axis_powers) leaders accused of war crimes. The [International Military Tribunal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Military_Tribunal), which sat in [Nuremberg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuremberg) and is often referred to as the "Nuremberg trials", prosecuted German leaders, including for [Nazi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nazi) perpetration of [The Holocaust](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Holocaust), while the [International Military Tribunal for the Far East](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Military_Tribunal_for_the_Far_East) in [Tokyo](30057.md) prosecuted Japanese leaders for war crimes and crimes against humanity.[^cite_note-8] In 1948 the [United Nations General Assembly](31957.md) first recognised the need for a permanent international court to deal with atrocities of the kind prosecuted after World War II.[^cite_note-iccfact-9] At the request of the General Assembly, the [International Law Commission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Law_Commission) (ILC) drafted two statutes by the early 1950s, but these were shelved during the [Cold War](325329.md), which made the establishment of an international criminal court politically unrealistic.[^cite_note-Cato-10]

[Benjamin B. Ferencz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Benjamin_B._Ferencz), an investigator of [Nazi war crimes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nazi_war_crime) after World War II and the chief prosecutor for the [United States Army](32087.md) at the [Einsatzgruppen trial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Einsatzgruppen_trial), became a vocal advocate of the establishment of international [rule of law](25166191.md) and of an international criminal court. In his book *Defining International Aggression: The Search for World Peace* (1975), he advocated for the establishment of such a court.[^cite_note-11] Another leading proponent was [Robert Kurt Woetzel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_Kurt_Woetzel), a German-born professor of international law, who co-edited *Toward a Feasible International Criminal Court* in 1970 and created the Foundation for the Establishment of an International Criminal Court in 1971.[^cite_note-12]

### Formal proposal and establishment

In June 1989, [A. N. R. Robinson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/A._N._R._Robinson), who would later become [Prime Minister of Trinidad and Tobago](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prime_Minister_of_Trinidad_and_Tobago), revived the idea of a permanent international criminal court by proposing the creation of a tribunal to address the [illegal drug trade](218449.md).[^cite_note-Cato-10][^cite_note-13] In response, the General Assembly tasked the ILC with once again drafting a statute for a permanent court.[^cite_note-ICCHistory-14][^cite_note-15]

While work began on the draft, the [UN Security Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UN_Security_Council) established two *ad hoc* tribunals in the early 1990s: the [International Criminal Tribunal for the former Yugoslavia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Criminal_Tribunal_for_the_former_Yugoslavia), created in 1993 in response to large-scale atrocities committed by armed forces during the [Yugoslav Wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yugoslav_Wars), and the [International Criminal Tribunal for Rwanda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Criminal_Tribunal_for_Rwanda), created in 1994 following the [Rwandan genocide](676659.md). The creation of these tribunals further highlighted to many the need for a permanent international criminal court.[^cite_note-ICCHistory-14][^cite_note-16]

In 1994, the ILC presented its final draft statute for the International Criminal Court to the General Assembly and recommended that a conference be convened to negotiate a treaty that would serve as the Court's statute.[^cite_note-17] To consider major substantive issues in the draft statute, the General Assembly established the Ad Hoc Committee on the Establishment of an International Criminal Court, which met twice in 1995.[^cite_note-\:2-18][^cite_note-\:3-19] After considering the Committee's report, the General Assembly created the Preparatory Committee on the Establishment of the ICC to prepare a consolidated draft text.[^cite_note-\:2-18][^cite_note-\:3-19] From 1996 to 1998, six sessions of the Preparatory Committee were held at the [United Nations headquarters](188955.md) in New York City, during which NGOs provided input and attended meetings under the umbrella organisation of the [Coalition for the International Criminal Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coalition_for_the_International_Criminal_Court) (CICC). In January 1998, the Bureau and coordinators of the Preparatory Committee convened for an inter-sessional meeting in [Zutphen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zutphen) in the Netherlands to technically consolidate and restructure the draft articles into a draft.[^cite_note-20]

Finally, the General Assembly convened a conference in Rome in June 1998, with the aim of finalising the treaty to serve as the Court's statute. On 17 July 1998, the [Rome Statute of the International Criminal Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rome_Statute_of_the_International_Criminal_Court) was adopted by a vote of 120 to seven, with 21 countries abstaining. The seven countries that voted against the treaty were [China](5405.md), [Iraq](7515928.md), [Israel](9282173.md), [Libya](17633.md), [Qatar](8238258.md), the U.S., and [Yemen](350939.md).[^cite_note-21]

&#x20;Israel's opposition to the treaty stemmed from the inclusion in the list of war crimes "the action of transferring population into occupied territory",[^cite_note-22] a provision added during the Rome Conference at the insistence of Arab countries with the specific intention of targeting Israeli citizens.[^cite_note-23]



The third Assembly of States Parties to the Rome Statute of the International Criminal Court is held in The Hague, the Netherlands in the Fall 2004.

The UN General Assembly voted on 9 December 1999 and again on 12 December 2000 to endorse the ICC.[^cite_note-ares54105-24][^cite_note-ares55155-25] Following 60 ratifications, the Rome Statute entered into force on 1 July 2002 and the International Criminal Court was formally established.[^cite_note-ai2002-26][^cite_note-27] The first bench of 18 judges was elected by the Assembly of States Parties in February 2003. They were sworn in at the inaugural session of the Court on 11 March 2003.[^cite_note-28]

The Court issued its first [arrest warrants](146746.md) on 8 July 2005,[^cite_note-LRA_warrants-2] and the first pre-trial hearings were held in 2006.[^cite_note-29]

The Court issued its first judgment in 2012 when it found Congolese rebel leader [Thomas Lubanga Dyilo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_Lubanga_Dyilo) guilty of war crimes related to [using child soldiers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Military_use_of_children).[^cite_note-\:5-3][^cite_note-30]

In 2010, the states parties of the Rome Statute held the first [Review Conference of the Rome Statute of the International Criminal Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Review_Conference_of_the_Rome_Statute_of_the_International_Criminal_Court) in [Kampala](56689.md), Uganda.[^cite_note-31] The Review Conference led to the adoption of two resolutions that amended the crimes under the jurisdiction of the Court. Resolution 5 amended Article 8 on war crimes, criminalising the use of certain kinds of weapons in non-international conflicts whose use was already forbidden in international conflicts. Resolution 6, pursuant to Article 5(2) of the Statute, provided the definition and a procedure for jurisdiction over the [crime of aggression](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crime_of_aggression).[^cite_note-32][^cite_note-33]

## Operation

The ICC began operations on 1 July 2002, upon the entry into force of the [Rome Statute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rome_Statute_of_the_International_Criminal_Court), a [multilateral treaty](9573984.md) that serves as the court's [charter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charter) and governing document. States which become party to the Rome Statute become members of the ICC, serving on the Assembly of States Parties, which administers the court. As of January 2025, there are [125 ICC member states](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/States_parties_to_the_Rome_Statute_of_the_International_Criminal_Court), 29 states have signed but not ratified the Rome Statute (including four who have withdrawn their signature) and 41 states have neither signed nor become parties to the Rome Statute.[^cite_note-34]

Intended to serve as the "court of last resort", the ICC complements existing national [judicial systems](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judicial_system) and may exercise its jurisdiction only when national courts are unwilling or unable to prosecute criminals.[^cite_note-35] It lacks universal territorial jurisdiction and may only investigate and prosecute crimes committed within member states, crimes committed by nationals of member states, or crimes in situations referred to the Court by the [UN Security Council](31956.md).[^cite_note-36][^cite_note-37][^cite_note-38]

The ICC held its first hearing in 2006, concerning war crimes charges against [Thomas Lubanga Dyilo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_Lubanga_Dyilo), a Congolese warlord accused of recruiting child soldiers; his subsequent conviction in 2012 was the first in the court's history. The Office of the Prosecutor has opened twelve [official investigations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Criminal_Court_investigations) and is conducting an additional nine preliminary examinations.

### Establishing the court's jurisdiction

The process to establish the court's jurisdiction may be "triggered" by any one of three possible sources: (1) a state party, (2) the Security Council or (3) a prosecutor. It is then up to the prosecutor acting [*proprio motu*](793396.md) to initiate an investigation under the requirements of [Article 15 of the Rome Statute](https://en.wikisource.org/wiki/Rome%20Statute%20of%20the%20International%20Criminal%20Court#Article_15:_Prosecutor). The procedure is slightly different when referred by a state party or the Security Council, in which cases the prosecutor does not need authorisation of the Pre-Trial Chamber to initiate the investigation. Where there is a reasonable basis to proceed, it is mandatory for the prosecutor to initiate an investigation. The factors listed in Article 53 considered for reasonable basis include whether the case would be admissible, and whether there are substantial reasons to believe that an investigation would not serve the interests of justice (the latter stipulates balancing against the gravity of the crime and the interests of the victims).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESchabas2011-39][^cite_note-40]

## Structure

The ICC is governed by the Assembly of States Parties, which is made up of the states that are party to the Rome Statute.[^cite_note-assembly-41] The Assembly elects officials of the Court, approves its budget, and adopts amendments to the Rome Statute. The Court itself has four organs: the Presidency, the Judicial Divisions, the Office of the Prosecutor, and the Registry.[^cite_note-structure-42]

### State parties

Main article: [States parties to the Rome Statute](6118062.md)

As of January 2025, 125 states[^cite_note-States-parties-and-others-43] are [parties to the Statute of the Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/States_parties_to_the_Rome_Statute_of_the_International_Criminal_Court), including all the countries of South America, nearly all of Europe, most of Oceania and roughly half of Africa.[^cite_note-UN_treaty_database-44][^cite_note-UN_treaty_database_12-2021-45] [Burundi](21490998.md) and the [Philippines](23440.md) were member states, but later withdrew effective 27 October 2017[^cite_note-BUwithdraw-46] and 17 March 2019,[^cite_note-47] respectively.[^cite_note-UN_treaty_database-44][^cite_note-UN_treaty_database_12-2021-45] A further 29 countries[^cite_note-States-parties-and-others-43] have signed but not [ratified](429650.md) the Rome Statute.[^cite_note-UN_treaty_database-44][^cite_note-UN_treaty_database_12-2021-45] The [law of treaties](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vienna_Convention_on_the_Law_of_Treaties) obliges these states to refrain from "acts which would defeat the object and purpose" of the treaty until they declare they do not intend to become a party to the treaty.[^cite_note-48] Four signatory states have informed the UN Secretary General that they no longer intend to become states parties and, as such, have no legal obligations arising from their signature of the Statute;[^cite_note-UN_treaty_database-44][^cite_note-UN_treaty_database_12-2021-45] they are Israel in 2002,[^cite_note-49] the [United States](4567589.md) on 6 May 2002,[^cite_note-50][^cite_note-51] Sudan on 26 August 2008,[^cite_note-52] and Russia on 30 November 2016.[^cite_note-53] Forty-one other states[^cite_note-States-parties-and-others-43] have neither signed nor acceded to the Rome Statute. Some of them, including [China](5405.md) and [India](14533.md), are critical of the Court.[^cite_note-54][^cite_note-55]&#x20;

#### Assembly

The Court's management oversight and legislative body, the Assembly of States Parties, consists of one representative from each state party.[^cite_note-RomeStatute-56]<sup>: article 112 </sup> Each state party has one vote and "every effort" has to be made to reach [decisions by consensus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Consensus_decision-making).[^cite_note-RomeStatute-56]<sup>: article 112 </sup> If consensus cannot be reached, decisions are made by vote.[^cite_note-RomeStatute-56]<sup>: article 112 </sup> The Assembly is presided over by a [president and two vice-presidents](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Presidents_and_Vice-Presidents_of_the_Assembly_of_States_Parties_of_the_International_Criminal_Court), who are elected by the members to three-year terms.

The Assembly meets in full session once a year, alternating between New York and [The Hague](30269.md), and may also hold special sessions where circumstances require.[^cite_note-RomeStatute-56]<sup>: article 112 </sup> Sessions are open to observer states and non-governmental organisations.[^cite_note-57]

The Assembly elects the judges and [prosecutors](440936.md), decides the Court's budget, adopts important texts (such as the Rules of Procedure and Evidence), and provides management oversight to the other organs of the Court.[^cite_note-assembly-41][^cite_note-RomeStatute-56]<sup>: article 112 </sup> Article 46 of the Rome Statute allows the Assembly to remove from office a judge or prosecutor who "is found to have committed serious misconduct or a serious breach of his or her duties" or "is unable to exercise the functions required by this Statute".[^cite_note-RomeStatute-56]<sup>: article 46 </sup>

The states parties cannot interfere with the judicial functions of the Court.[^cite_note-iccnow-asp-58] Disputes concerning individual cases are settled by the Judicial Divisions.[^cite_note-iccnow-asp-58]

##### Bureau

The Bureau of the Assembly assists the Assembly in discharging its responsibilities between the Assembly's plenary sessions. The Assembly elects a Bureau consisting of a President, two Vice-Presidents, and 18 members elected for three-year terms, chosen with regard to equitable geographical distribution and adequate representation of the principal legal systems of the world.[^cite_note-RomeStatute-56]<sup>: article 112(3) </sup> The Bureau meets as often as necessary (typically several times a year) and handles preparatory and administrative work, oversight, and coordination so that the full Assembly—which meets only once a year—can function effectively. It also operates through subsidiary working groups, notably The Hague Working Group and the New York Working Group.[^cite_note-RomeStatute-56]<sup>: article 112(4) </sup>

### Organs

The ICC has four principal organs: the Presidency, the Judicial Divisions, the Office of the Prosecutor and the Registry.[^cite_note-59][^cite_note-60]

- The President is the most senior judge chosen by the eighteen judges in the Judicial Division.

- The Judicial Division is composed of eighteen judges and hears cases before the Court.

- The Office of the Prosecutor is headed by the Prosecutor, who investigates crimes and initiates criminal proceedings before the Judicial Division.

- The Registry is headed by the Registrar and is charged with managing all the administrative functions of the ICC, including the headquarters, detention unit, and public defence office.

The ICC employs over 900 personnel from roughly 100 countries and conducts proceedings in English and French.[^cite_note-61]

#### Presidency

Main article: [Presidency of the International Criminal Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presidency_of_the_International_Criminal_Court)



[Tomoko Akane](65855794.md), President of the ICC since 2024

The [Presidency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/President_of_the_International_Criminal_Court) is responsible for the proper administration of the Court (apart from the Office of the Prosecutor).[^cite_note-presidency-62] It comprises the President and the First and Second Vice-Presidents—three judges of the Court who are elected to the Presidency by their fellow judges for a maximum of two three-year terms.[^cite_note-RomeStatute-56]<sup>: article 38 </sup>

[Song Sang-hyun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Song_Sang-hyun) was the President of the ICC from 2009 to 2015. As of March 2024, the President is [Tomoko Akane](65855794.md) from [Japan](15573.md), who took office on 11 March 2024, succeeding [Piotr Hofmański](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Piotr_Hofma%C5%84ski). Her first term will expire in 2027.[^cite_note-New_ICC_Presidency_elected_for_2024-2027-63]

#### Judicial Division

Main article: [Judges of the International Criminal Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judges_of_the_International_Criminal_Court)

The Judicial Divisions consist of the 18 judges of the Court, organised into three chambers—the Pre-Trial Chamber, Trial Chamber and Appeals Chamber—which carry out the judicial functions of the Court. Judges are elected to the Court by the Assembly of States Parties. They serve nine-year terms and are not generally eligible for re-election.[^cite_note-chambers-64] All judges must be nationals of states parties to the Rome Statute, and no two judges may be nationals of the same state.[^cite_note-RomeStatute-56]<sup>: article 36 </sup> They must be "persons of high moral character, impartiality and integrity who possess the qualifications required in their respective States for appointment to the highest judicial offices".[^cite_note-RomeStatute-56]<sup>: article 36 </sup>

The Prosecutor or any person being investigated or prosecuted may request the disqualification of a judge from "any case in which his or her impartiality might reasonably be doubted on any ground".[^cite_note-RomeStatute-56]<sup>: article 41 </sup> Any request for the disqualification of a judge from a particular case is decided by an absolute majority of the other judges.[^cite_note-RomeStatute-56]<sup>: article 41 </sup> Judges may be removed from office if "found to have committed serious misconduct or a serious breach of his or her duties" or is unable to exercise his or her functions.[^cite_note-RomeStatute-56]<sup>: article 46 </sup> The removal of a judge requires both a two-thirds majority of the other judges and a two-thirds majority of the states parties.[^cite_note-RomeStatute-56]<sup>: article 46 </sup>

#### Office of the Prosecutor

Main article: [Prosecutor of the International Criminal Court](33903993.md)



ICC prosecutors [Fatou Bensouda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fatou_Bensouda) and [Luis Moreno Ocampo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luis_Moreno_Ocampo), with [Estonia's Minister of Foreign Affairs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minister_of_Foreign_Affairs_\(Estonia\)), [Urmas Paet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urmas_Paet), in 2012

The Office of the Prosecutor (OTP) is responsible for conducting investigations and prosecutions.[^cite_note-prosecutor-65] It is headed by the Prosecutor of the International Criminal Court, who is assisted by one or more Deputy Prosecutors.[^cite_note-structure-42] The Rome Statute provides that the Office of the Prosecutor shall act independently;[^cite_note-RomeStatute-56]<sup>: article 42 </sup> as such, no member of the Office may seek or act on instructions from any external source, such as states, [international organisations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_organisation), non-governmental organisations or individuals.[^cite_note-prosecutor-65]

The Prosecutor may open an investigation under three circumstances:[^cite_note-prosecutor-65]

- when a situation is referred by a state party;

- when a situation is referred by the [United Nations Security Council](31956.md), acting to address a threat to international peace and security; or

- when the Pre-Trial Chamber authorises the prosecutor to open an investigation on the basis of information received from other sources, such as individuals or non-governmental organisations.

Any person being investigated or prosecuted may request the disqualification of a prosecutor from any case "in which their impartiality might reasonably be doubted on any ground".[^cite_note-RomeStatute-56]<sup>: article 42 </sup> Requests for the disqualification of prosecutors are decided by the Appeals Chamber.[^cite_note-RomeStatute-56]<sup>: article 42 </sup> A prosecutor may be removed from office by an absolute majority of the states parties through a finding "to have committed serious misconduct or a serious breach of his or her duties" or is unable to exercise his or her functions.[^cite_note-RomeStatute-56]<sup>: article 46 </sup>



[Karim Khan](11606957.md), then ICC Prosecutor, photographed in April 2022

Lead prosecutor Luis Moreno Ocampo of [Argentina](18951905.md), in office from 2003 to 2012, was succeeded in the role by [Fatou Bensouda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fatou_Bensouda) of [Gambia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gambia), who served from 16 June 2012 to 16 June 2021.[^cite_note-66] (She was elected to the nine-year term on 12 December 2011.)[^cite_note-prosecutor-65]

On 12 February 2021, British barrister [Karim Khan](11606957.md) was selected in a secret ballot against three other candidates to serve as lead prosecutor as of 16 June 2021. As a British barrister, Khan had headed the United Nations' special investigative team when it looked into [Islamic State](9087364.md) crimes in Iraq. At the ICC, he had been lead defence counsel on cases from Kenya, Sudan and Libya.[^cite_note-67]

##### Policy papers

The Office of the Prosecutor occasionally publishes policy papers that put forth the considerations given to topics the office focuses on, and often the criteria for case selection.[^cite_note-68] While a policy paper does not give the Court jurisdiction over a new category of crimes, it promises what the Office of Prosecutor will consider when selecting cases in the upcoming term of service. OTP's policy papers are subject to revision.[^cite_note-69]

The following papers have been published since the start of the ICC:

- 1 September 2007: Policy Paper on the Interest of Justice

  [^cite_note-70]

- 12 April 2010: Policy Paper on Victims' Participation

  [^cite_note-71]

- 1 November 2013: Policy Paper on Preliminary Examinations

  [^cite_note-72]

- 20 June 2014: Policy Paper on Sexual and Gender-Based Crimes

  [^cite_note-73]

- 15 September 2016: Policy paper on case selection and prioritisation

  [^cite_note-74]

- 15 November 2016: Policy on Children

  [^cite_note-75]

The paper published in September 2016 announced that the ICC will focus on environmental crimes when selecting cases.[^cite_note-BowcottVidal-76] The Office will give particular consideration to prosecuting Rome Statute crimes that are committed by means of, or that result in, "inter alia, the destruction of the environment, the illegal exploitation of natural resources or the illegal dispossession of land".[^cite_note-77] This has been interpreted as a major shift in environmental law[^cite_note-78][^cite_note-BowcottVidal-76] and a move with significant effects.[^cite_note-79][^cite_note-80]

#### Registry

The Registry is responsible for the non-judicial aspects of the administration and servicing of the Court.[^cite_note-registry-81] This includes, among other things,

> The administration of legal aid matters, court management, victims and witnesses matters, defence counsel, detention unit, and the traditional services provided by administrations in international organisations, such as finance, translation, building management, procurement and personnel.[^cite_note-registry-81]

The Registry is headed by the Registrar, who is elected by the judges to a five-year term.[^cite_note-structure-42] As of April 2023 the Registrar is [Osvaldo Zavala Giler](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Osvaldo_Zavala_Giler).[^cite_note-osvaldo-82]

| Registrar | Term | Reference |

## Prosecutable crimes

The Court's subject-matter jurisdiction means the crimes for which individuals can be prosecuted. Individuals can only be prosecuted for crimes that are listed in the Statute. The primary crimes are listed in article 5 of the Statute and defined in later articles: [genocide](12441.md) (defined in article 6), [crimes against humanity](61099.md) (defined in article 7), [war crimes](48626.md) (defined in article 8), and [crimes of aggression](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crime_of_aggression) (defined in article 8 *bis*) (since 2018).[^cite_note-RomeStatute-56]<sup>: art. 5 </sup> In addition, article 70 defines *offences against the administration of justice*, which is a fifth category of crime for which individuals can be prosecuted.[^cite_note-87]

### Genocide

Article 6 defines the crime of genocide as "acts committed with [intent to destroy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genocidal_intent), in whole or in part, a national, ethnical, racial or religious group".[^cite_note-RomeStatute-56]<sup>: art. 6 </sup> There are five such acts which constitute crimes of genocide under Article 6:[^cite_note-RomeStatute-56]<sup>: arts. 6(a)–6(e) </sup>

1. Killing members of a group

2. Causing serious bodily or mental harm to members of the group

3. Deliberately inflicting on the group conditions of life calculated to bring about its physical destruction

4. Imposing measures intended to prevent births within the group

5. Forcibly transferring children of the group to another group

The definition of these crimes is identical to those contained within the [Convention on the Prevention and Punishment of the Crime of Genocide](143988.md) of 1948.

### Crimes against humanity

Article 7 defines crimes against humanity as acts "committed as part of a widespread or systematic attack directed against any civilian population, with knowledge of the attack".[^cite_note-RomeStatute-56]<sup>: art. 7 </sup> The article lists 16 such individual crimes:[^cite_note-Elements_of_Crimes-88]

1. [Murder](20171.md)

   [^cite_note-89]

2. Extermination

   [^cite_note-90]

3. [Enslavement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Enslavement)

   [^cite_note-91]

4. [Deportation or forcible transfer of population](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Population_transfer#Changes_in_international_law)

   [^cite_note-92]

   [^cite_note-93]

5. Imprisonment or other severe deprivation of physical liberty

6. [Torture](47702.md)

7. [Rape](7980471.md)

8. [Sexual slavery](173634.md)

9. [Enforced prostitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Enforced_prostitution)

10. [Forced pregnancy](37569084.md)

11. [Enforced sterilisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Enforced_sterilization)

12. [Sexual violence](731637.md)

13. [Persecution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persecution)

14. [Enforced disappearance of persons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forced_disappearance)

15. [Apartheid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apartheid_\(crime\))

16. Other inhumane acts

### War crimes

Article 8 defines war crimes depending on whether an armed conflict is either international (which generally means it is fought between states) or non-international (which generally means that it is fought between non-state actors, such as rebel groups, or between a state and such non-state actors). The most serious crimes constitute either [grave breaches](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geneva_Conventions#Enforcement) of the [Geneva Conventions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geneva_Conventions) of 1949 and its Protocols.<sup>: art. 8(2)(c) </sup> In total there are 74 war crimes listed in article 8.[^cite_note-Elements_of_Crimes-88]

Eleven crimes constitute grave breaches of the Geneva Conventions and apply only to international armed conflicts:[^cite_note-Elements_of_Crimes-88]

1. Wilful killing

2. Torture

3. Inhumane treatment

4. Biological experiments

5. Wilfully causing great suffering

6. Destruction and appropriation of property

7. Compelling service in hostile forces

8. Denying a fair trial

9. Unlawful deportation and transfer

10. Unlawful confinement

11. Taking hostages

Seven crimes constitute serious violations of Article 3 common to the Geneva Conventions and apply only to non-international armed conflicts:[^cite_note-Elements_of_Crimes-88]

1. Murder

2. Mutilation

3. Cruel treatment

4. Torture

5. Outrages upon personal dignity

6. Taking hostages

7. Sentencing or execution without due process

Another 56 crimes are defined by Article 8: 35 apply to international armed conflicts and 21 to non-international armed conflicts.[^cite_note-Elements_of_Crimes-88] Such crimes include attacking civilians or civilian objects, attacking peacekeepers, causing excessive incidental death or damage, transferring populations into occupied territories, treacherously killing or wounding, denying quarter, pillaging, employing poison, using expanding bullets, rape and other forms of sexual violence, and conscripting or using child soldiers.[^cite_note-RomeStatute-56]<sup>: art. 8 </sup>

### Crimes of aggression

Article 8 *bis* defines [crimes of aggression](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crimes_of_aggression). The Statute originally provided that the Court could not exercise its jurisdiction over the crime of aggression until such time as the states parties agreed on a definition of the crime and set out the conditions under which it could be prosecuted.[^cite_note-iccfact-9][^cite_note-RomeStatute-56]<sup>: art. 5 </sup> Such an amendment was adopted at the first review conference of the ICC in Kampala, Uganda, in June 2010. This amendment specified that the ICC would not be allowed to exercise jurisdiction of the crime of aggression until two further conditions had been satisfied: (1) the amendment has entered into force for 30 states parties and (2) on or after 1 January 2017, the Assembly of States Parties has voted in favour of allowing the Court to exercise jurisdiction. On 26 June 2016 the first condition was satisfied[^cite_note-94] and the state parties voted in favour of allowing the Court to exercise jurisdiction on 14 December 2017.[^cite_note-asp.icc-cpi.int-95] The Court's jurisdiction to prosecute crimes of aggression was accordingly activated on 17 July 2018.[^cite_note-asp.icc-cpi.int-95]

The Statute, as amended, defines the crime of aggression as "the planning, preparation, initiation or execution, by a person in a position effectively to exercise control over or to direct the political or military action of a State, of an act of aggression which, by its character, gravity and scale, constitutes a manifest violation of the [Charter of the United Nations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations_Charter)."[^cite_note-RomeStatute-56]<sup>: art. 8 *bis*(1) </sup> The Statute defines an "act of aggression" as "the use of armed force by a State against the sovereignty, territorial integrity or political independence of another State, or in any other manner inconsistent with the Charter of the United Nations."[^cite_note-RomeStatute-56]<sup>: art. 8 *bis*(2) </sup>[^cite_note-96] The article also contains a list of seven acts of aggression, which are identical to those in [United Nations General Assembly Resolution 3314](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations_General_Assembly_Resolution_3314) of 1974 and include the following acts when committed by one state against another state:[^cite_note-RomeStatute-56]<sup>: art. 8 *bis*(2)(a)–8 *bis*(2)(g) </sup>

1. Invasion or attack by armed forces against territory

2. [Military occupation](816214.md) of territory

3. [Annexation](18950961.md) of territory

4. [Bombardment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bombardment) against territory

5. Use of any weapons against territory

6. [Blockade](293054.md) of ports or coasts

7. Attack on the land, sea, or air forces or marine and air fleets

8. The use of armed forces which are within the territory of another state by agreement, but in contravention of the conditions of the agreement

9. Allowing territory to be used by another state to perpetrate an act of aggression against a third state

10. Sending armed bands, groups, [irregulars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irregular_military), or [mercenaries](73374.md) to carry out acts of armed force

### Offences against the administration of justice

Article 70 criminalises certain intentional acts which interfere with investigations and proceedings before the Court, including giving false testimony, presenting false evidence, corruptly influencing a witness or official of the Court, retaliating against an official of the Court, and soliciting or accepting bribes as an official of the Court.[^cite_note-RomeStatute-56]<sup>: art. 70 </sup>

## Jurisdiction and admissibility

The Rome Statute requires that several criteria exist in a particular case before an individual can be prosecuted by the Court. The Statute contains three jurisdictional requirements and three admissibility requirements. All criteria must be met for a case to proceed. The three jurisdictional requirements are (1) subject-matter jurisdiction (what acts constitute crimes), (2) territorial or personal jurisdiction (where the crimes were committed or who committed them), and (3) temporal jurisdiction (when the crimes were committed).

For an individual to be prosecuted by the Court either territorial jurisdiction or personal jurisdiction must exist. Therefore, an individual can only be prosecuted if he or she has either (1) committed a crime within the territorial jurisdiction of the Court or (2) committed a crime while being a national of a state that is within the territorial jurisdiction of the Court.

### Territorial jurisdiction

The [territorial jurisdiction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Territorial_jurisdiction) of the Court includes the territory, registered vessels, and registered aircraft of states which have either (1) become party to the Rome Statute or (2) accepted the Court's jurisdiction by filing a declaration with the Court.[^cite_note-RomeStatute-56]<sup>: article 12 </sup>

In situations that are referred to the Court by the United Nations Security Council, the territorial jurisdiction is defined by the Security Council, which may be more expansive than the Court's normal territorial jurisdiction.[^cite_note-RomeStatute-56]<sup>: article 13(b) </sup> For example, if the Security Council refers a situation that took place in the territory of a state that has both not become party to the Rome Statute and not lodged a declaration with the Court, the Court will still be able to prosecute crimes that occurred within that state.

### Personal jurisdiction

The personal jurisdiction of the Court extends to all natural persons who commit crimes in the territories of states that are parties to the Rome Statute or of states that have accepted the Court's jurisdiction by filing a declaration with the Court, as well as on board aircraft or vessels registered with those states.[^cite_note-RomeStatute-56]<sup>: article 12 (2. a) </sup> Personal jurisdiction may always be exercised over nationals of all the above-mentioned states, regardless of where the crimes were committed.[^cite_note-RomeStatute-56]<sup>: article 12 (2. b) </sup> As with territorial jurisdiction, the personal jurisdiction can be expanded by the Security Council if it refers a situation to the Court.[^cite_note-RomeStatute-56]<sup>: article 13(b) </sup>

### Temporal jurisdiction requirements

&#x20;Temporal jurisdiction is the time period over which the Court can exercise its powers. No statute of limitations applies to any of the crimes defined in the Statute.[^cite_note-RomeStatute-56]<sup>: article 29 </sup> This is not completely retroactive. Individuals can only be prosecuted for crimes that took place on or after 1 July 2002, which is the date that the Rome Statute entered into force.[^cite_note-RomeStatute-56]<sup>: article 11(1) </sup> If a state became party to the Statute, and therefore a member of the Court, after 1 July 2002, then the Court cannot exercise jurisdiction prior to the membership date for certain cases.[^cite_note-RomeStatute-56]<sup>: article 11(2) </sup> For example, if the Statute entered into force for a state on 1 January 2003, the Court could only exercise temporal jurisdiction over crimes that took place in that state or were committed by a national of that state on or after 1 January 2003.

### Admissibility requirements

To initiate an investigation, the Prosecutor must (1) have a "reasonable basis to believe that a crime within the jurisdiction of the Court has been or is being committed", (2) the investigation would be consistent with the principle of complementarity, and (3) the investigation serves the interests of justice.[^cite_note-RomeStatute-56]<sup>: article 53(1) </sup>

The principle of complementarity means the Court will only prosecute an individual if states are unwilling or unable to prosecute.[^cite_note-97] Therefore, if legitimate national investigations or proceedings into crimes have taken place or are ongoing, the Court will not initiate proceedings. This principle applies regardless of the outcome of national proceedings.[^cite_note-RomeStatute-56]<sup>: article 17(a)–17(c) </sup> Even if an investigation is closed without any criminal charges being filed or if an accused person is acquitted by a national court, the Court will not prosecute an individual for the crime in question so long as it is satisfied that the national proceedings were legitimate. The application of the complementarity principle has recently come under theoretical scrutiny.[^cite_note-98][^cite_note-RomeStatute-56]<sup>: article 17(a)–17(c) </sup>

The Court will only initiate proceedings if a crime is of "sufficient gravity to justify further action by the Court".[^cite_note-RomeStatute-56]<sup>: article 17(d) </sup>

The Prosecutor will initiate an investigation unless there are "substantial reasons to believe that an investigation would not serve the interests of justice" when "\[t]aking into account the gravity of the crime and the interests of victims".[^cite_note-RomeStatute-56]<sup>: article 53(1)(c) </sup> Furthermore, even if an investigation has been initiated and there are substantial facts to warrant a prosecution and no other admissibility issues, the Prosecutor must determine whether a prosecution would serve the interests of justice "taking into account all the circumstances, including the gravity of the crime, the interests of victims and the age or infirmity of the alleged perpetrator, and his or her role in the alleged crime".[^cite_note-RomeStatute-56]<sup>: article 53(2)(c) </sup>

### Individual criminal responsibility

The Court has jurisdiction over natural persons.[^cite_note-RomeStatute-56]<sup>: article 25(1) </sup> A person who commits a crime within the jurisdiction of the Court is individually responsible and liable for punishment in accordance with the Rome Statute.[^cite_note-RomeStatute-56]<sup>: article 25(2) </sup> In accordance with the Rome Statute, a person shall be criminally responsible and liable for punishment for a crime within the jurisdiction of the Court if that person: Commits such a crime, whether as an individual, jointly with another or through another person, regardless of whether that other person is criminally responsible;[^cite_note-RomeStatute-56]<sup>: article 25(3)(a) </sup> Orders, solicits or induces the commission of such a crime which in fact occurs or is attempted;[^cite_note-RomeStatute-56]<sup>: article 25(3)(b) </sup> For the purpose of facilitating the commission of such a crime, aids, abets or otherwise assists in its commission or its attempted commission, including providing the means for its commission;[^cite_note-RomeStatute-56]<sup>: article 25(3)(c) </sup> In any other way contributes to the commission or attempted commission of such a crime by a group of persons acting with a common purpose.[^cite_note-RomeStatute-56]<sup>: article 25(3)(d) </sup> In respect of the crime of genocide, directly and publicly incites others to commit genocide;[^cite_note-RomeStatute-56]<sup>: article 25(3)(e) </sup> Attempts to commit such a crime by taking action that commences its execution by means of a substantial step, but the crime does not occur because of circumstances independent of the person's intentions[^cite_note-RomeStatute-56]<sup>: article 25(3)(f) </sup>

## Procedure

### Trial

Trials are conducted under a hybrid [common law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Common_law) and [civil law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Civil_law_\(legal_system\)) judicial system, but it has been argued the procedural orientation and character of the court is still evolving.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESchabas2011302-99][^cite_note-100] A majority of the three judges present, as [triers of fact](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trier_of_fact) in a [bench trial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bench_trial), may reach a decision, which must include a full and reasoned statement.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESchabas2011322-101] Trials are supposed to be public, but proceedings are often closed, and such exceptions to a public trial have not been enumerated in detail.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESchabas2011303–304-102] *[In camera](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/In_camera)* proceedings are allowed for protection of witnesses or defendants as well as for confidential or sensitive evidence.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESchabas2011304-103] [Hearsay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hearsay) and other indirect evidence is not generally prohibited, but it has been argued the court is guided by hearsay exceptions which are prominent in common law systems.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESchabas2011312-104] There is no [subpoena](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Subpoena) or other means to compel witnesses to come before the court, although the court has some power to compel testimony of those who chose to come before it, such as fines.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESchabas2011316-105]

### Rights of the accused

The Rome Statute provides that all persons are [presumed innocent until proven guilty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presumption_of_innocence) beyond [reasonable doubt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Legal_burden_of_proof),[^cite_note-RomeStatute-56]<sup>: article 66 </sup> and establishes certain rights of the accused and persons during investigations.[^cite_note-106][^cite_note-107] These include the right to be fully informed of the charges against them; the right to have a lawyer appointed, free of charge; the right to a [speedy trial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Speedy_trial); and the right to examine the witnesses against them.

To ensure "equality of arms" between defence and prosecution teams, the ICC has established an independent Office of Public Counsel for the Defence (OPCD) to provide logistical support, advice and information to defendants and their counsel.[^cite_note-108][^cite_note-109] The OPCD also helps to safeguard the rights of the accused during the initial stages of an investigation.[^cite_note-110] Thomas Lubanga's defence team said they were given a smaller budget than the prosecutor and that evidence and witness statements were slow to arrive.[^cite_note-cfr-111]

### Victim participation

One of the great innovations of the Statute of the International Criminal Court and its Rules of Procedure and Evidence is the series of rights granted to victims.[^cite_note-112][^cite_note-113] For the first time in the history of international criminal justice, victims have the possibility under the Statute to present their views and observations before the Court.

Participation before the Court may occur at various stages of proceedings and may take different forms, although it will be up to the judges to give directions as to the timing and manner of participation.

Participation in the Court's proceedings will in most cases take place through a legal representative and will be conducted "in a manner which is not prejudicial or inconsistent with the rights of the accused and a fair and impartial trial".

The victim-based provisions within the Rome Statute provide victims with the opportunity to have their voices heard and to obtain, where appropriate, some form of reparation for their suffering. It is the aim of this attempted balance between [retributive](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Retributive_justice) and [restorative justice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Restorative_justice) that, it is hoped, will enable the ICC to not only bring criminals to justice but also help the victims themselves obtain some form of justice. Justice for victims before the ICC comprises both procedural and substantive justice, by allowing them to participate and present their views and interests, so that they can help to shape truth, justice and reparations outcomes of the Court.[^cite_note-114]

Article 43(6) establishes a Victims and Witnesses Unit to provide "protective measures and security arrangements, counselling and other appropriate assistance for witnesses, victims who appear before the Court, and others who are at risk on account of testimony given by such witnesses."[^cite_note-RomeStatute-56]<sup>: article 43(6) </sup> Article 68 sets out procedures for the "Protection of the victims and witnesses and their participation in the proceedings."[^cite_note-RomeStatute-56]<sup>: article 68 </sup> The Court has also established an Office of Public Counsel for Victims, to provide support and assistance to victims and their legal representatives.[^cite_note-2006report-115]

### Reparations

Victims before the International Criminal Court can also claim reparations under Article 75 of the Rome Statute. [Reparations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reparation_\(legal\)) can only be claimed when a defendant is convicted and at the discretion of the Court's judges.[^cite_note-116] So far the Court has ordered reparations against [Thomas Lubanga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_Lubanga).[^cite_note-117] Reparations can include compensation, restitution and rehabilitation, but other forms of reparations may be appropriate for individual, collective or community victims. Article 79 of the Rome Statute establishes a Trust Fund to provide assistance before a reparation order to victims in a situation or to support reparations to victims and their families if the convicted person has no money.[^cite_note-118]

### Cooperation by states not party to Rome Statute

One of the principles of international law is that a treaty does not create either obligations or rights for third states without their consent, and this is also enshrined in the 1969 [Vienna Convention on the Law of Treaties](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vienna_Convention_on_the_Law_of_Treaties).[^cite_note-119] The cooperation of the non-party states with the ICC is envisioned by the [Rome Statute](68762.md) of the International Criminal Court to be of a voluntary nature.[^cite_note-RomeStatute-56]<sup>: article 87 (5)(a) </sup> States not acceded to the Rome Statute might still be subject to an obligation to cooperate with the ICC in certain cases.[^cite_note-zhu-120] When a case is referred to the ICC by the UN [Security Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Security_Council) all UN member states are obliged to cooperate, since its decisions are binding for all of them.[^cite_note-121] Also, there is an obligation to respect and ensure respect for international humanitarian law, which stems from the [Geneva Conventions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geneva_Conventions) and [Additional Protocol I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Additional_Protocol_I),[^cite_note-122] which reflects the absolute nature of [international humanitarian law](1093604.md).[^cite_note-123]

In relation to cooperation in investigation and evidence gathering, it is implied from the Rome Statute[^cite_note-RomeStatute-56]<sup>: article 99 </sup> that the consent of a non-party state is a prerequisite for [the Prosecutor](#Office_of_the_Prosecutor) to conduct an investigation within its territory, and it seems that it is even more necessary for him to observe any reasonable conditions raised by that state, since such restrictions exist for states party to the Statute.[^cite_note-zhu-120] Taking into account the experience of the [International Criminal Tribunal for the former Yugoslavia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Criminal_Tribunal_for_the_former_Yugoslavia) (which worked with the principle of primacy, instead of [complementarity](#Complementarity)) in relation to cooperation, some scholars have expressed their pessimism as to the possibility of the ICC obtaining the cooperation of non-party states.[^cite_note-zhu-120] As for the actions that the ICC can take toward non-party states that do not cooperate, the Rome Statute stipulates that the Court may inform the [Assembly of States Parties](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assembly_of_States_Parties) or Security Council, when the matter was referred by it, when a non-party state refuses to cooperate after it has entered into an *ad hoc* arrangement or an agreement with the Court.[^cite_note-RomeStatute-56]<sup>: article 87(5) </sup>

### Amnesty and national reconciliation processes

It is unclear to what extent the ICC is compatible with reconciliation processes that grant [amnesty](18940624.md) to human rights abusers as part of agreements to end conflict.[^cite_note-dworkin-124] Article 16 of the Rome Statute allows the Security Council to prevent the Court from investigating or prosecuting a case,[^cite_note-RomeStatute-56]<sup>: article 16 </sup> and Article 53 allows the Prosecutor the discretion not to initiate an investigation if he or she believes that "an investigation would not serve the interests of justice".[^cite_note-RomeStatute-56]<sup>: article 53 </sup> Former ICC president [Philippe Kirsch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philippe_Kirsch) has said that "some limited amnesties may be compatible" with a country's obligations genuinely to investigate or prosecute under the Statute.[^cite_note-dworkin-124]

It is sometimes argued that amnesties are necessary to allow the [peaceful transfer of power](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peaceful_transition_of_power) from abusive regimes. By denying states the right to offer amnesty to human rights abusers, the International Criminal Court may make it more difficult to negotiate an end to conflict and a transition to democracy. For example, the outstanding arrest warrants for four leaders of the [Lord's Resistance Army](17873743.md) are regarded by some as an obstacle to ending the insurgency in Uganda.[^cite_note-125][^cite_note-126] Czech politician Marek Benda argues that "the ICC as a deterrent will in our view only mean the worst dictators will try to retain power at all costs".[^cite_note-127] The United Nations[^cite_note-128] and the [International Committee of the Red Cross](5023832.md)[^cite_note-129] maintain that granting amnesty to those accused of war crimes and other serious crimes is a violation of international law.

## Facilities

### Headquarters

| International Criminal Court |  |
| --- | --- |
|  |  |
| [Map](14880.md) |  |
| General information |  |
| Status | Completed |
| Type | Office |
| Location | [The Hague](30269.md), Netherlands |
| Coordinates | [52°6′20″N 4°19′4″E  /  52.10556°N 4.31778°E  / 52.10556; 4.31778](https://geohack.toolforge.org/geohack.php?pagename=International_Criminal_Court\&params=52_6_20_N_4_19_4_E_region\:NL-ZH_type\:landmark) |
| Construction started | Autumn 2012 |
| Opened | December 2015 |
| Technical details |  |
| Floor area | 52,000 m<sup>2</sup> (560,000 sq ft) |
| Design and construction |  |
| Architect | [Schmidt Hammer Lassen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Schmidt_Hammer_Lassen) |
| Developer | Combination Visser & Smit Bouw and Boele & van Eesteren ('Courtys') |

The official seat of the Court is in [The Hague](30269.md), Netherlands, but its proceedings may take place anywhere.[^cite_note-RomeStatute-56]<sup>: article 3 </sup>[^cite_note-131]

The Court moved into its first permanent premises in The Hague, located at Oude Waalsdorperweg 10, on 14 December 2015.[^cite_note-132] Part of The Hague's International Zone,[^cite_note-133] which also contains the [Peace Palace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peace_Palace), [Europol](525329.md), [Eurojust](1059460.md), [ICTY](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ICTY), [OPCW](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/OPCW) and [The Hague World Forum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Forum_Convention_Center), the court facilities are situated on the site of the *Alexanderkazerne*, a former military barracks, adjacent to the dune landscape on the northern edge of the city. The ICC's detention centre is a short distance away.

The land and financing for the construction were provided by the Netherlands.[^cite_note-Permanent-134] The host state also organised and financed the architectural design competition, which started at the end of 2008. Three architects were chosen by an international jury from 171 applicants to enter into further negotiations. The Danish firm [Schmidt Hammer Lassen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Schmidt_Hammer_Lassen) were selected to design the premises, since its design met all the ICC criteria, such as design quality, sustainability, functionality and costs.[^cite_note-135]



The former (provisional) headquarters of the ICC, in use until December 2015

The barracks were demolished from November 2011 to August 2012.[^cite_note-136] In October 2012 the tendering procedure for the general contractor was completed and the combination Visser & Smit Bouw and Boele & van Eesteren ("Courtys") was selected.[^cite_note-137]

The building has a compact footprint and consists of six connected building volumes with a garden motif. The tallest volume with a green façade, placed in the middle of the design, is the Court Tower, which accommodates three courtrooms. The rest of the building's volumes accommodate the offices of the different organs of the ICC.[^cite_note-138]

From 2002 to late 2015, the ICC was housed in interim premises in The Hague provided by the Netherlands.[^cite_note-139] Formerly belonging to [KPN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/KPN), the provisional headquarters were located at Maanweg 174 in the east-central portion of the city.

### Detention centre

Main article: [People detained by the International Criminal Court § Detention centre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/People_detained_by_the_International_Criminal_Court#Detention_centre)

The ICC's detention centre accommodates both those convicted by the court and serving sentences as well as those suspects detained pending the outcome of their trial. It comprises twelve cells on the premises of the [Scheveningen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scheveningen) branch of the [Hague Penitentiary Institution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hague_Penitentiary_Institution), The Hague, close to the ICC's headquarters in the Alexanderkazerne.[^cite_note-Scheveningen-140][^cite_note-141] Suspects held by the former [International Criminal Tribunal for the former Yugoslavia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Criminal_Tribunal_for_the_former_Yugoslavia) were held in the same prison and shared some facilities, like the fitness room, but had no contact with suspects held by the ICC.[^cite_note-Scheveningen-140]

### Other offices

The ICC maintains a liaison office in New York[^cite_note-NY-142] and field offices in places where it conducts its activities.[^cite_note-field-offices-143] As of 18 October 2007, the Court had field offices in [Kampala](56689.md), [Kinshasa](45773.md), [Bunia](1925095.md), [Abéché](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ab%C3%A9ch%C3%A9) and [Bangui](56617.md).[^cite_note-field-offices-143]

### Digital infrastructure

The ICC used [Microsoft](19001.md) digital infrastructure, including email, [Microsoft Office](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Microsoft_Office), file storage and "software for evidence analysis" through to 2025.[^cite_note-NYT_EU_growing_fear_US_tech-144] During 2025, following [Trump sanctions against the ICC](4567589.md#Second_presidency_of_Donald_Trump), the ICC Prosecutor and some other ICC staff switched from Microsoft email to [Proton Mail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proton_Mail).[^cite_note-NYT_EU_growing_fear_US_tech-144][^cite_note-145]

In late October 2025, the ICC stated that it would switch from Microsoft Office to [OpenDesk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/OpenDesk), a [free and open-source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free_and_open-source) productivity software suite managed by the German [Centre for Digital Sovereignty in Public Administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ZenDiS) (ZenDiS).[^cite_note-146][^cite_note-147]

The ICC is financed by contributions from the states parties. The amount payable by each state party is determined using the same method as the United Nations:[^cite_note-budget_resolution_ICC-ASP-6-20-149] each state's contribution is based on the country's capacity to pay, which reflects factors such as national income and population. The maximum amount a single country can pay in any year is limited to 22% of the Court's budget; Japan paid this amount in 2008.

The Court spent €80.5 million in 2007.[^cite_note-budget-performance-2007-150] The Assembly of States Parties approved a budget of €90.4 million for 2008,[^cite_note-budget_resolution_ICC-ASP-6-20-149] €101.2 million for 2009,[^cite_note-budget_resolution_2009-151] and €141.6 million for 2017.[^cite_note-court-today-152] As of April 2017, the ICC's staff consisted of 800 persons from approximately 100 states.[^cite_note-court-today-152]

## Trial history

See also: [List of people indicted in the International Criminal Court](18248714.md)

The Court's Pre-Trial Chambers have [publicly indicted](18248714.md) 73 people. Proceedings against 38 are ongoing: 32 are at large as fugitives, two cases are in the pre-trial stage, one person is on trial, and three cases are in the appeals stage. Proceedings against 36 have been completed: three are serving sentences, seven have finished sentences, four have been acquitted, eight have had the charges against them dismissed, five have had the charges against them withdrawn, and nine have died before the conclusion of the proceedings against them.

The indicted individuals include Ugandan rebel leader [Joseph Kony](420855.md), former President [Omar al-Bashir](393625.md) of [Sudan](27421.md), President [Uhuru Kenyatta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uhuru_Kenyatta) of [Kenya](188171.md), Libyan head of state [Muammar Gaddafi](53029.md), President [Laurent Gbagbo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Laurent_Gbagbo) of [Ivory Coast](57744.md) and former Vice President [Jean-Pierre Bemba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jean-Pierre_Bemba) of the [Democratic Republic of the Congo](76762.md).

In 2015, several Congolese individuals were tried by the ICC. [Thomas Lubanga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_Lubanga) was convicted and sentenced to 14 years imprisonment, [Germain Katanga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Germain_Katanga) to 12 years, and [Mathieu Ngudjolo Chui](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mathieu_Ngudjolo_Chui) was acquitted.[^cite_note-lubanga-153][^cite_note-154]

The judgment of [Jean-Pierre Bemba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jean-Pierre_Bemba) was rendered in March 2016. Bemba was convicted on two counts of crimes against humanity and three counts of war crimes. This marked the first time the ICC convicted someone of sexual violence as they added rape to his conviction.[^cite_note-155][^cite_note-156] Bemba's convictions were overturned by the Court's Appeal Chamber in June 2018.[^cite_note-157] The Court refused to compensate Bemba for losses suffered by him during his 10 years of imprisonment. It has been argued that this decision raises important questions about the court's present powers.[^cite_note-158]

*[Ntaganda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bosco_Ntaganda)* (DR Congo) was convicted to 30 years for crimes against humanity. The *[Bemba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jean-Pierre_Bemba) et al.* OAJ case and the *[Laurent Gbagbo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Laurent_Gbagbo)-[Blé Goudé](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_Bl%C3%A9_Goud%C3%A9)* trial in the Côte d'Ivoire situation ended in acquittals. The *[Banda](32321707.md)* trial in the situation of Darfur, Sudan, was scheduled to begin in 2014 but the start date was vacated.

Charges against [Malian](19127.md) [Ahmad al-Faqi al-Mahdi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ahmad_al-Faqi_al-Mahdi) have been confirmed[^cite_note-159] and he was sentenced to nine years in prison. On 25 November 2021, his sentence was commuted to 7 years in prison, and he was released on 18 September 2022.[^cite_note-160] [Ugandan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ugandan) [Dominic Ongwen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dominic_Ongwen) has been convicted to a prison sentence of 25 years.[^cite_note-161]

On 6 July 2020, two [Uyghur](31783.md) activist groups filed a complaint with the ICC calling for it to investigate PRC officials for [crimes against Uyghurs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persecution_of_Uyghurs_in_China), including allegations of genocide.[^cite_note-NYT-ICC-162][^cite_note-Guardian-ICC-163] In December 2020, ICC prosecutors rejected the complaint, stating that the ICC did not have jurisdictional basis to proceed.[^cite_note-164]

On 31 October 2023, the Israeli families of over 34 victims of the [2023 Hamas-led attack on Israel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_Hamas-led_attack_on_Israel), filed an Article 15 communication with the ICC prosecutor's office urging an investigation into the killings and abductions, and the ICC confirmed the receipt of the filing.[^cite_note-165] [Reporters Without Borders](526054.md) also lodged a complaint regarding the deaths of eight Palestinian journalists in the [Gaza Strip](12047.md) during Israel's bombardment, as well as an Israeli journalist killed during a surprise attack by Hamas in southern Israel.[^cite_note-166]



[Maria Lvova-Belova](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maria_Lvova-Belova) during a meeting with Russian President [Vladimir Putin](32817.md) on March 9, 2022

On 17 March 2023, ICC judges issued [arrest warrants](73313153.md) for Russian president [Vladimir Putin](32817.md) and the [Presidential Commissioner for Children's Rights in Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Children's_ombudsman#Russia) [Maria Lvova-Belova](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maria_Lvova-Belova)[^cite_note-icc_judges_2023_03_17_reuters-167][^cite_note-issues_warrant_2023_03_17_reuters-168][^cite_note-Khan_statement_Putin_LvovaBelova_17March2023-169][^cite_note-situation_2023_03_17_icc-170] for [child abductions in the 2022 Russian invasion of Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Child_abductions_in_the_2022_Russian_invasion_of_Ukraine). Russia denounced the arrest warrants as "outrageous".[^cite_note-situation_2023_03_17_icc-170][^cite_note-putin_warrant_2023_03_18_bbc-171] Putin became the first head of state of a [U.N. Security Council Permanent Member](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Permanent_members_of_the_United_Nations_Security_Council) to be the subject of an ICC arrest warrant.[^cite_note-issues_warrant_2023_03_17_reuters-168] Although Russia withdrew its signature from the Rome Statute in 2016, and is thus not a participant in the ICC nor under its jurisdiction, Putin can be charged for actions against Ukraine, which only became a full state party to the Statute on 1 January 2025, because it had previously accepted the Court's jurisdiction for an indefinite period starting from 21 November 2013. Should Putin travel to a state party, he can be arrested by local authorities.[^cite_note-putin_warrant_2023_03_18_bbc-171] Later in 2023, [Russia's Ministry of Internal Affairs](53056.md) retaliated by placing several ICC officials on its wanted list.[^cite_note-ClaudiaChiappa-172][^cite_note-173][^cite_note-174] In March 2024, the ICC issued two more arrest warrants, for [Sergey Kobylash](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sergey_Kobylash), the commander of the [Long-Range Aviation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Long_Range_Aviation) of the [Russian Aerospace Forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Aerospace_Forces), and [Viktor Sokolov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Viktor_Sokolov_\(naval_officer\)), the commander of the [Black Sea Fleet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_Sea_Fleet) over their role in war crimes in Ukraine.[^cite_note-175] In December 2025, the [Moscow City Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moscow_City_Court) sentenced the chief prosecutor [Karim Khan](11606957.md), the first vice president [Rosario Salvatore Aitala](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rosario_Salvatore_Aitala) and seven others judges of the ICC in absentia to prison terms of up to 15 years each.[^cite_note-176]

On 20 May 2024, the ICC's chief prosecutor Karim Khan announced his intention to seek arrest warrants for Israeli prime minister [Benjamin Netanyahu](56472.md), Israeli defence minister [Yoav Gallant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yoav_Gallant), leader of Hamas [Yahya Sinwar](53195047.md), leader of the Al Qassem Brigades [Mohammed Deif](92534.md), and Hamas political leader [Ismail Haniyeh](41207463.md) in connection to [war crimes committed in the Israel-Hamas war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_crimes_in_the_Israel-Hamas_war).[^cite_note-177][^cite_note-178][^cite_note-179][^cite_note-180][^cite_note-181] On 21 November, warrants were formally issued for Netanyahu, Gallant and Deif.[^cite_note-182][^cite_note-183] Warrants for Haniyeh and Sinwar were withdrawn following confirmation of their deaths in July and October respectively.[^cite_note-184] In February 2025, ICC canceled arrest warrant issued against Hamas’s late military chief Muhammad Deif, who was killed in Gaza in July 2024.[^cite_note-185]

On 23 January 2025, the ICC's chief prosecutor Karim Khan announced requests for arrest warrants against Taliban leader [Haibatullah Akhundzada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haibatullah_Akhundzada) and the [chief justice of Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chief_Justice_of_Afghanistan) [Abdul Hakim Haqqani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abdul_Hakim_Haqqani), for crimes against humanity of the oppression and persecution of Afghan women and girls, who have been deprived of the freedom of movement, the rights to control their bodies, to education, and to a private and family life. Alleged resistance and opposition are brutally suppressed with murder, imprisonment, torture, rape, and other forms of sexual violence, since 2021.[^cite_note-Taliban_warrent-186] On 8 July 2025, the ICC's Pre-Trial Chamber II issued the requested arrest warrants.[^cite_note-187]

## Investigations and preliminary examinations

Main article: [International Criminal Court investigations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Criminal_Court_investigations)

Map of countries in which the ICC is currently investigating situations.

ICC investigations and examinations, as of March 2022\
&#x20;

&#x20; Official investigations (Uganda, DR Congo, Central African Republic I + II, Darfur (Sudan), Kenya, Libya, Côte d'Ivoire, Mali, Georgia, Burundi, Afghanistan, Palestine, Venezuela I, Bangladesh/Myanmar, Philippines, Ukraine)

&#x20; Authorisation to open investigation requested (none at present)

&#x20; Ongoing preliminary examinations (Nigeria)

&#x20; Closed preliminary examinations that have not resulted in an investigation (Colombia, Iraq, Honduras, South Korea, Comoros (registered vessels), Gabon, Bolivia, Guinea)

By 2025, the Office of the Prosecutor has opened investigations in [Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Criminal_Court_investigation_in_Afghanistan), [Bangladesh/Myanmar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Criminal_Court_investigation_in_Bangladesh/Myanmar), Burundi, the Central African Republic (twice), Côte d'Ivoire, [Darfur in Sudan](33596946.md), the [Democratic Republic of the Congo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Criminal_Court_investigation_in_the_Democratic_Republic_of_the_Congo), [Georgia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Criminal_Court_investigation_in_Georgia), [Kenya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Criminal_Court_investigation_in_Kenya), [Libya](60488397.md), Lithuania/Belarus, [Mali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Criminal_Court_investigation_in_Mali), [Palestine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Criminal_Court_investigation_in_Palestine), the [Philippines](68742643.md), [Uganda](32447500.md), [Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Criminal_Court_investigation_in_Ukraine), and [Venezuela I](62526848.md).[^cite_note-188] The Office of the Prosecutor carried out and closed preliminary investigations in Bolivia; Colombia; Congo II; Gabon; Guinea; Honduras; [Iraq/the United Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Criminal_Court_and_the_2003_invasion_of_Iraq); registered vessels of Comoros, Greece, and Cambodia; South Korea; and Venezuela II.[^cite_note-ICC_preliminary_investigations_template-189][^cite_note-190] An ongoing preliminary examination is being carried out in the situation in Nigeria.[^cite_note-ICC_preliminary_investigations_template-189]
